## Phase door

Phase door was a conjuration or spell that opened passages, where there were none.

Effects
While the spell was called phase "door", what appeared on casting was an ethereal, and thus invisible, tunnel. This tunnel was both invisible and unusable for everyone but the caster, though the caster can open access to others.

Only on wooden, plaster or stone surfaces was the tunnel successfully created. The caster can walk through the wall via this tunnel for an increasing number of times per strength of the caste. By using up more uses, the caster can also bring others along. Light or sound from the one side didn't get to the other side and if the spell was forcefully ended, for example through dispelling, those inside the phase door were ejected out as it happened when a passwall spell was dispelled with someone inside.

Access for usage could be given to others by stating a number of conditions on casting. These conditions needed to be observable, like identity or passwords.

While its ethereal nature made it possible for some creatures to see it, like with a gem of true seeing, just seeing didn't allow usage.

A phase door collapsed when its uses were completely depleted. This could be circumvented by making it permanent through a permanency spell, thus allowing infinite uses of the phase door.

Components
Only verbal components were required to cast this spell.

History
The spell was attributed to Netherese arcanist Undine in  and was originally called Undine's door.

## Phindar streeaka

Phindar streeaka, meaning "mindless recklessness", was counted among the drow fighting styles but wasn't truly a technique. It was a catch-all phrase to describe berserker-like combat, and it lacked any kind of underlying order in its approach to combat.

Description
As the name "mindless recklessness" suggested, the fighting "style" was about mindless violence. Being used by berserkers, it lacked the concept of finesse, and all "skills" they used had nothing to do with intellectual combat theory but everything to do with brute force. This made some moves of the phindar streeaka "style" similar to the z'ress a'thalak style but unlike the former the latter was a product of real combat training.

Training
The style was used by drow berserkers. They didn't train in anything in particular.

Reputation
The "style" was used by the few berserkers in drow society and it was unpopular, because of its complete lack of finesse and its utter chaos was considered something of an unwelcome mirror on the true face of drow society as a whole.

Only primitive and/or small drow communities sported a meaningful number of users, but it was popular among the followers of Ghaunadaur.

Appendix
Background
Although unmentioned, this style is based on the Rage ability of the Barbarian class in 3.0-edition Dungeons & Dragons.

## Phylornel

Phylornel, also known as Buoyance, was a floating city of Netheril. Unlike many other such enclaves, it rarely moved from its location over the Netheril river, and was suspended only a short distance from the ground. When Karsus's Folly occurred in, Phylornel fell into the river and was destroyed, but several of its citizens survived the relatively minor fall.

Geography
The enclave was situated along the Netheril river, several miles south of the Narrow Sea.

Description
At the preference of its ruling archwizard, Buoyance, Phylornel floated roughly 200 feet above the surface—uncommonly low for a floating enclave. The city itself, crowned by several towers, was organized into a series of concentric circles, with the more important structures and personalities closer to the center. At the very center lay the personal quarters of Buoyance.

History
Phylornel was created by the archwizard Buoyance in . Buoyance was a master of inventive magic, and spent most of his time maneuvering—with varying degrees of subtlety—against other powerful archwizards. Despite this, Phylornel remained stable and relatively stationary for most of its history. Buoyance eventually achieved lichdom, allowing him to rule his enclave for over a thousand years with no successors.

When the archwizard Karsus's casting of Karsus's avatar resulted in the catastrophic failure of all magic, Phylornel fell to the ground alongside the other enclaves of Netheril. Although the city's towers were destroyed, roughly 150 residents survived Phylornel's fall. Buoyance himself was also rumored to have survived.

Notable inhabitants
 Buoyance, the tyrannical archwizard-turned-lich who founded and ruled Phylornel.

## Phintarn

Phintarn, nicknamed "Redblade", was the lord of the town of Llorkh in the Savage Frontier in the 1350s DR.

History
Phintarn was the last of the old lords of Llorkh. These were a succession of human rulers, often retired miners or warriors who'd earned the respect of the community who, it was said, would never have tolerated the Zhentarim in their town. However, shortly before the Year of the Prince, 1357 DR,The date of this unknown. Waterdeep and the North (dated 1357 DR) only says this was "recently", implying sometime in 1357 DR or shortly before. Phintarn was found dead at the foot of the Lord's Keep. He'd been murdered by the Zhentarim; this was unproved but widely suspected by the people.

Overnight, the mage Geildarr Ithym seized the Lord's Keep and claimed the throne. Soon after, four hundred "Lord's Men" arrived and in a handful of battles slaughtered all of the existing militia under the pretext that they were trouble-makers. The citizens found themselves under the "protection" of the Lord's Men. Thus, Llorkh was quickly and quietly conquered by the Zhentarim and under the control of their puppet.

Shortly after, Phintarn's brother died mysteriously one night, and his inn was razed the very next day.

## Pholruth

The Pholruth was a multi-function building owned by the nagra (local law enforcement) of Delzimmer in southeast Faerûn circa the Year of Wild Magic, 1372 DR. It combined an inn, a barracks, a police station, a jail, a stable, and a bank called the Shrimmer.

Location
The Pholruth was located near the heart of Delzimmer.

Structure
Rising up three stories with wings radiating from a central structure, the Pholruth was an imposing stone edifice. There were three unconnected cellars used for different purposes.

Interior
One wing of the ground floor contained the main nagra station house with its accompanying jail in the cellar below. Another wing on the first floor housed the combined bank, moneychanging service, and ethrael (property deeds) office known as the Shrimmer. Its cellar was used as vaults for deposits and the ethrael records for land, buildings, and large non-perishable items. Another wing was used as a stable for the nagra and for guests of the inn. The third cellar was used for storing goods seized from those who broke the kauladd (the local code of law).

The second floor of the Pholruth was given over to offices and sleeping quarters for the nagra and kala. The kala were the hired judges that supervised the Shrimmer and held court when needed.

The top floor served as an inn for outlanders doing business or passing through Delzimmer. It was quite clean and safe—being in the same building complex as the nagra station and possibly off-duty officers—but fairly expensive.

Services

See the main article for a description of how law enforcement worked and how the ethrael system functioned.

The inn cost three gold pieces per night and meals were not included. To stable a mount cost an extra five silver pieces per animal per night.

Appendix
References

## Phlambror's

Phlambror's was a tavern in Khôltar that catered primarily to a dwarven clientele.

Location
Starting at Handrornlar (the north gate out of Khôltar) and walking southwest down Orntathtar Way, the first building on the right (northwest) was the inn called Taurgaur's Tarjteir, and then immediately adjacent to the inn was Phlambror's.

Atmosphere
By the Year of Wild Magic, 1372 DR, Phlambror's was a raucous tavern where dwarves felt at home, with loud singing, brawling, telling of tall tales, and drinking. Other races were not turned away, and many would bring in a tankard to have it filled and then depart, but most non-dwarves were not entirely comfortable being in the company of drunken dwarves that were just as likely to be belligerent and gloomy as boisterous and jolly.

Services
Among the strong drinks that could be purchased here was a particularly sweet and thick liquor, called garlraw, that most non-dwarves could not stomach. If a patron became incapacitated, the staff would kindly clean them up and either carry them to their room at the Tarjteir next door, or put them in the tavern's loft, known as the "groan-and-snore", to sleep it off.

History
Phlambror was the name of the previous owner, a handsome human that was a notorious womanizer. His philandering days were cut short by a jealous husband, but in his will the lady's man forgave all his debtors and left all his property investments to the stakeholders. This included his tavern, then named The Hand of Welcome, which he bequeathed to three dwarves that frequented the bar for many hours whenever they were in Khôltar. The new owners renamed the establishment after him and it became a favorite "home away from home" for dwarves visiting from the Great Rift.

## Pharaoh

"Pharaoh" was the term used for the ultimate ruler of the empire of Mulhorand. The title was also used in the land of Raurin for the leaders of Bakar.

Divinity
Up until the Time of Troubles, the pharaohs of Mulhorand were physical incarnations of Horus-Re, the solar deity who bore the title of "Pharaoh of the Gods".  The pharaoh's family members were also considered incarnations, though of the other deities of the Mulhorandi pantheon. The exception to this was the firstborn son. They would also be an incarnation of Horus-Re, such that the title of pharaoh could be passed down to him.

Requirements
All of Mulhorand's pharaohs were required to be male. They were also expected to be members of the House of Helcaliant, the priestly House of Horus-Re, and thus descendants of an incarnation of the god.

Responsibilities

The pharaoh of Mulhorand ruled from his throne in the city of Skuld. While the most powerful individual in Mulhorand in name, most pharaohs allowed the priests to determine most of its policies, as the Church of Horus-Re was tightly intertwined with Mulhorand's governing institutions. The official second-in-command under the pharaoh was the vizier, who was the highest-ranking cleric in the Church of Horus-Re.

When a new pharaoh took the throne, typically, a census would be his first act.

Burial
In both the nations of Bakar and Mulhorand, pharaohs were buried in elaborate tombs. In both nations, it was believed by the populace that the bodies of their pharaohs were prepared for a journey into the afterlife, as if a continuation of life rather than an end.

In Mulhorand, a pharaoh's family was traditionally buried with them and their tombs were situated in the Land of the Dead, part of the valley Asanibis on the edge of the Dragonsword Mountains, near the small town of Mishtan. The construction of these were overseen by the Osiran clergy that governed Mishtan. Being the "Guardian of the Dead" in Mulhorandi faith, dedicated shrines to Osiris were typically placed within these tombs.

History
Long after the kingdom of Bakar had fallen to ruin, legends of its pharaohs still circulated in the Raurin Desert. According to some, the pharaohs of Bakar were evil, while others claimed they were largely benevolent rulers.

The only pharaohs in the history of Mulhorand who were not an incarnation of Horus-Re were two incarnations of Thoth who ruled for a short time after the loss of Thay. This period of Mulhorand's history lasted from 922 DR to 955 DR.

Following the Time of Troubles, the overgod Ao finally dispersed the Imaskari planar barrier that separated the Mulhorandi gods' manifestations from their divine essences in the Outer Planes. As a result of their reunion, the then-pharaoh Horustep III and all his family members lost the fragments of divinity that were previously within them, reducing them to mere mortals with divine lineage.

Notable Pharaohs
 Amun-Re, the last pharaoh of Bakar.
 Horusret I, the first pharaoh of Mulhorand. His reign lasted from -1045 DR to -926 DR.
 Akonhorus II, pharaoh in 1320 DR
 Horuseres II, the fifth pharaoh and the one whose tomb was the greatest of any other pharaoh.
 Horustep III, the sixteenth pharaoh of Mulhorand and the first to be a mortal rather than an incarnation of a god.
 Ramenhorus III, the twelfth pharaoh of Mulhorand. His reign lasted from 955 DR to 1098 DR, dying from the forces of Thay in their first attempted invasion.
 Sorenhotep I, a pharaoh in the waning years of the Second Empire, married to the fey Queen Serenia.
 Thothibistep I, the ninth pharaoh of Mulhorand and the first to be the physical incarnation of a god other than Horus-Re.
 Thothibistep II, the tenth pharaoh of Mulhorand and the second to last pharaoh to be an incarnation of the god Thoth.

## Phlambror

Phlambror was a wealthy merchant, investor, and business owner in Khôltar, the Iron City. He was also a notorious philanderer and was eventually killed by a jealous husband.

Description
Phlambror was tall and exceedingly handsome.

Personality
He was very sophisticated, a friend to many, kind to others, and charitable to the destitute regardless of race or occupation.

Possessions
Phlambror had many investments in businesses around town and was the sole owner of a tavern called The Hand of Welcome located on Orntathtar Way next to Taurgaur's Tarjteir near Handrornlar, the northern gate through the walls surrounding the Iron City.

History
Phlambror was an astute businessman with a weakness for women and he was equally good at negotiations for business or pleasure. His womanizing finally caught up to him when a cuckolded husband killed him in a jealous rage. Even though the murdering spouse was arrested standing over Phlambror's body and covered in his blood, the man was never prosecuted because at least four hundred other men with wives and daughters turned themselves in, claiming to have done the deed. The garthraun (police) had no choice but to dismiss the crime as justified.

Phlambror had never married and had no known heirs or kin, so the reading of his will before the Belarkh (ruling lord) was of concern to many of his debtors. To the surprise of all, he nullified all debts owed him and all his stakes in businesses reverted back to their owners. His many properties were bequeathed to various citizens and, in particular, his tavern was donated to three dwarves that were regular customers whenever they came to town. The three promptly retired from their other businesses, renamed the tavern Phlambror's in his honor, and made it a "home away from home" for Great Rift dwarves that came through Khôltar.

References

## Phangarl

The Phangarl family was wealthy and influential in Khôltar, south Faerûn, circa the Year of Wild Magic, 1372 DR. They were one of twenty Houses that had a seat on the Onsruur—the council of lords that governed the Iron City.

Organization
This bloodline had no obvious distinguishing features. As a clan, they enjoyed acting, especially comedy, and enthusiastically supported playwrights and troupes that wrote and performed humorous works.

Activities
In Khôltar, the Phangarls owned a hinge-making company, several coach manufacturers, and some businesses that specialized in decoration. Foreign investments included various trading companies in Var the Golden. They also bred horses for sale, but were not equestrians by any means.

Tactics
There was nothing funny about their trading tactics. The Phangarl clan secretly dealt with Shaaryan raiders, paying them to attack caravans belonging to or being used by their rivals, while leaving costers in which they had an interest alone.

He was ultimately captured and polymorphed into the helpless and humiliating form of a wingless wonder. He was presumed to remain trapped like this by the mid–14 century DR.

## Phoedele

Phoedele was a Zhentarim mercenary warlord in the Dalelands around 1479 DR.

History
In 1479 DR, Phoedele was hired by Barcero, a Sembian wizard agent of the shadovar, to recover the  Diamond Staff of Chomylla, which was in possession of Imani, a sage from Turmish residing at Hap.

For this task, she hired some Fangfist orcs.

However, when Phoedele had the staff in her hands she decided to betray Barcero and went in search of the Uvaeren treasures for herself.

Phoedele and her group went to the Uvaeren ruins in Mistledale and there Phoedele was on the verge of opening a powerful selu'kiira using the Staff when her party was attacked by some Cult of the Dragon minions of Dretchroyaster.

Personality
Phoedele always had the best interests of the Zhents in mind and her goal was to help the Zhentarim to dominate the Heartlands like it had in the past.

## Philaspur Olósynne

King Philaspur was the fourth monarch of the Olósynne Dynasty of Aglarond in the late 12 century DR. He reigned from 1194 DR to his death in battle in 1197 DR.

History
Following the death of his elder brother, King Elthond, in the Battle of the Singing Sands against the Red Wizards of Thay in the, Philaspur succeeded him as king of Aglarond.

He died in the, during the Battle of Brokenhands against the Thayan people.

Relationships
He was the son of Althon "the Old" and the younger brother of Elthond.

His daughters, who would rule the nation after his death, were the twins Thara and Ulae, more commonly known as the Gray Sisters.

## Phantyni Evanara

Phantyni Evanara was a gold elf priestess of Labelas Enoreth from the elven realm of Siluvanede. She was a member of House Evanara.

She died and became an undying baelnorn. By 1370 DR, she had served her god for millennia at the Temple Beyond Time in Mhiilamniir in neighboring Eaerlann.

Alongside Susklahava Orbryn and Roanmara Neirdre, she was one of the Timespinners: historians, oracles, sages, and seers whose advice was sought by the faithful on the temple's occasional returns to the normal time-stream.

## Phlegethon

Phlegethon was the third layer of Pandemonium. It contained the divine realms of Diirinka and Ibrandul, and was one of the locations of the Unseelie Court in some versions of the Great Wheel cosmology.

Geography
Unlike the other layers of Pandemonium, Phlegethon's gravity was normally oriented. This trait, together with the humidity of the place, created vast and complex limestone stalagmite and stalactite formations that were constantly battered by the incessant winds. The tunnels were dominated by the sound of wind mixed with dripping water.

The darkness that permeated the entire plane was also stronger in Phlegethon, and the layer was considerably colder than the rest of the plane. The rocks in its tunnel walls absorbed light and radiative heat, both natural and magic, so all light sources within the layer only shone at half capacity. Even darkvision was impaired within the layer.

Rumors & Legends
Rumors existed of overgrown dragons making their lairs throughout the layer. They seemed to be reinforced by the large bounties of treasure that adventurers sometimes stumbled upon while visiting the caverns.

Notable Locations
 Hidden Betrayal, realm of Diirinka.
 Ibrandyllaran, realm of Ibrandul
 The Unseelie Court, realm of the Queen of Air and Darkness outside of the Feywild.

 Windglum, a town located in one of the largest caverns, and whose inhabitants were wary and suspicious of all travelers. Its largest tavern, the Scaly Dog inn, was a common meeting place for visitors.

Inhabitants
The layer was said to be inhabited by large ferocious creatures such as albino cave wolves, giant crayfish, and enormous oozes. The Unseelie fey who inhabited the Court entirely focused their efforts into overthrowing the Seelie Court, also by judging whether captured visitors could prove useful to their cause.

## Phlegethos

Phlegethos was the fourth layer of the Nine Hells of Baator. Ruled by the archdevils Belial and Fierna, it was the center of the Nine Hells' judicial system, where devils and mortals alike navigated the impossibly intricate legalities of Baator and faced both the ecstatic highs of victory and the excruciating lows of defeat. The Heart of Flames, sometimes titled of the Flame, was the most classically infernal of the Nine Hells, a fiery desolation of officiated debauchery and damnation.

Description
Phlegethos was best described as a brutal expanse of giant volcanoes, a tortured, nightmare landscape of fire, lava, and heat antithetical to most forms of life. Here the powers of fire were enhanced, while those of water and cold were diminished and extremely difficult to use. Any creature without some resistance to fire present in Phlegethos would be quickly immolated, and its structures were designed to withstand the intense heat and occasional redirected lava flow.

Phlegethos was a plane that existed to burn. It was nearly identical to the Elemental Plane of Fire, moreso than any other place in the Great Wheel cosmology, distinguished by the greater presence of pain and malevolence. It was one of the most visited of Baator's layers, the legendary archetype most often envisioned by those of the Material Plane when thinking of the horrors of the Nine Hells (not realizing that the other eight were equally horrible).

Geography
The landscape of Phlegethos was bleak, featuring gouting fires, hills of ash, pits of smoking dung, and massive volcanoes underneath a dark, starless void. The volcanoes expelled rivers of lava that flowed into bubbling pools and seas of molten magma, which brewed hurricanes of hot wind, choking smoke and pyroclastic ash. So permeated by heat was Phlegethos that the ground was always uncomfortably hot, and the air temperature high enough to char meat and ripple the air with distortion.

Ironically for the most archetypal Hell, Phlegethos had the most chaotic and concealing physical environment in the Nine Hells. It was a violent land where ground tremors were common and eruptions occurred often, where jets of flame spouted from a million fissures and it was not unusual for more to suddenly open in the scalding, crystalline earth, and where long, worm-like volcanic tubes in the rock and other formations provided plentiful hiding spaces. Though the volcanic activity made the plane more dangerous, to the point it occasionally destroyed objects in mid-air, it also made the plane rich in mineral wealth. All major metals (including the metals unique to the Hells, arjale and tantulhor) could be found in Phlegethos; obsidian and diamonds were abound in hardened lava-flows, and rubies, sapphires, zircons, spinels, tourmalines and lazurite could be found under the earth. Here was made much of the mundane weaponry of the Hells, which Belial was paid handsomely for in treasure, services, and favors.

Despite its fiery nature, there were parts of Phlegethos with moisture. The plane's outlands were reeking fens which, like Phlegethos's neighbor Minauros, were wetter and lower than the interior. There was even a kind of plant life on the layer, the fire grapes, from which the somewhat subtle  known as "infernal wine" was distilled.

Fire
While some of the burning flows of Phlegethos were ones of lava, many were instead literal rivers of fire. They ran across the 4 layer in liquid form, burning any who entered them as if they had just emerged into the Plane of Elemental Fire, into lakes, of which there were at least two large ones where they blazed brightest. The rivers were responsible for much of the traffic to Phlegethos, as magic-users often visited the plane's fabled "firefalls", natural forges where the fire tumbled from volcanic ridges down into clefts, due to reports that the flames in such spots were a potent element in the creation of magic items; supposedly the best flame tongues and helms of brilliance were made there. All these bodies of burning liquid were interconnected.

Beyond their magical utility, the fires of Phlegethos were evidently supernatural in another, more commonly expressed way: they were nearly sentient. The animated flames danced and wandered through the air, providing weird lighting to Phlegethos's otherwise empty sky. Furthermore, the fires had a purpose: to burn the unwary and unworthy. Seeking tendrils of fire leaped at and curved unnaturally towards those not expressly authorized by the baatezu to be in Phlegethos. They appeared attracted to fast movements and scorched intruders mercilessly, seemingly taking delight in setting things aflame, coiling their victims until they were charred corpses or until a fiend took over. Conversely, not only were Nine Hells natives left unharmed by the spiteful flames, but even those who were susceptible to fire suffered neither injury nor pain from exposure to them.

Cosmography
By Asmodeus's decree, no planar portals could connect directly to any layer of Hell besides Avernus. This meant that in general, if one wanted to get to Phlegethos they would have to go the layer above it, Minauros, and find a portal, and likewise would need another to get to Stygia below. Portals existed from the Sinking City of Minauros to the only city in Phlegethos, Abriymoch, as well as from Abriymoch to It was also possible to reach Phlegethos from the lowest points of Minauros by way of constant dribbles of slimy water that sluiced into long and generally lethal falls. The Elemental Plane of Earth could be reached from Phlegethos using a diamond (of any size) as a portal key.

World Axis
In the World Axis cosmology model, the Nine Hells were a planet-shaped astral dominion floating in the Astral Sea, no longer of infinite size nor consisting of layers. In this cosmology Phlegethos was a long, twisting cavern about  long,   wide, and  high. It sat far deeper than Minauros, and was reached by descending down the dripping steps cut into the stinking fumaroles of the Endless Bog for miles on end until one finally emerged from the steep stairway at the feet of great rocky columns in a black plain of cooling lava.

This Phlegethos was also a layer of fiery destruction, from the volcanoes rising from the cavern floor to the cascades of lava pouring down from fissures in the ceiling, forming slag-splattered rivers and great lakes of molten rock. To leave, one had to follow the Road of Cinders, a torturous, broken maze of old lava tubes and rifts that ran from the depths of Abyrimoch into the darkness below. The road was also dangerous, prone as it was to sudden lava flow spills, but eventually it grew colder and darker until it emerged on the Stygian shores. In contrast to the Minauros, Phlegethos and Stygia sat at roughly the same depth, the difference in space between them being horizontal.

Notable Locations
 Abriymoch, called the Mount of Leaping Flames, the only city of Phlegethos and the strongest fortress of the middle Hells. The city of obsidian and crystal was located in the caldera of Phlegethos's largest volcano,The status of Abriymoch's volcano has changed over the years. Dragon #75 (p. 26) claimed the volcano was dead, and Planes of Law ("Baator", p. 19) claimed it had only occasional eruptions. As of Guide to Hell (p. 32) the volcano was described as fully active, and Fiendish Codex II: Tyrants of the Nine Hells (p. 52) described its cauldron as being filled with molten lava. the volcanic heart of Phlegethos. It was from a palace there that Belial and Fierna ruled the city, but it was generally governed by the pit fiend Gazra from his manor of crystal statues within. The laws governing the city allowed it to function as a kind of carnival, with numerous infernal taverns, casinos, theatres, and other venues. It was also the home of the Diabolical Court, an independent institution under Belial's supervision that handled Baatorian legal disputes.

 Fierna's palace, a lithe, sinuously stretching tower of crystal rock wreathed in blue flame which dominated the landscape of Phlegethos for miles around its ring of sputtering magma pools. Inside, a burning staircase spiraled down into Fierna's pleasure domes, deep caverns each dedicated to a specific vice. Floor grates provided easy access to the warren of prison cells below, allowing Fierna to jab at captives (including the likes of paladins and unicorns) with a lance at her leisure. Many cells were reserved for Fierna's ex-lovers, allowing her current paramours to see the growing ranks of their predecessors, which rarely led them to question their own fate.

 The Hall of One Thousand Sighs and Screams, a place of elegant marble halls and bejeweled decor at the heart of Fierna's palace. None but Fierna and her closest companions were allowed within, for it was where Belial was sequestered. It was where he spent all his time, and where he personally taught his daughter all he knew of the "arts of love and pain".

 Tymphalos, also called the Mouth of Iron, a massive foundry worked by lesser devils and hundreds of duergar to forge weapons and infernal constructs for Hell, housed inside a volcano and supervised by the archdevil Tymphal.

Divine Realms
 Aeaea, the divine realm of Hecate of the Olympian pantheon. Her realm was much like the rest of Phlegethos with its charred land and rivers of fire, but volcanoes extended to the horizon, and in one of the craters was a throne of obsidian studded in fire opal where Hecate sat. The environment mirrored her mood, volcanoes exploding in response to her displeasure and flames flaring over a dozen feet high when she sighed. Eventually this realm was cited to exist in Minauros, but her hellish homes were ultimately secondary, the greater version of Aeaea being located in the Gray Waste.

 Jealous Heart, the divine realm of Inanna of the Untheric pantheon. The realm was a vast field of crimson dust featuring sweet fruit trees fed by rivers of blood. It was not as physically hot as the rest of the layer, but might appear that way given the realm's red tint, and awakened an equally hot passion for both battle and intimacy in its visitors and inhabitants. When Hecate's realm was stated to be in Phlegethos, hers was adjacent to it, and both had suitable conduits to reach the worlds where they were venerated.

Pit of Flame
The Pit of Flame, also called the Lake of Fire, exemplified Phlegethos and Hell overall; it was a place of pain and punishment but also of pleasure and purification that blurred the lines between the two so heavily that it was difficult to tell where either ended. Located in the center of Phlegethos, the Pit was a -wide lake of boiling filth and excreta that jettisoned columns of white flame over a hundred feet into the air. At first glance, this would seem harmless to the devils, most if not all of which were immune to fire, but not even devils could endure the agonizing flames of the pit. That was because the flames were not ordinary fire, but hellfire, a kind of unspeakably hot energy drawn from Hell itself that caused even those normally immune to extreme heat to writhe and convulse in unimaginable torment.

Osyluths, with the exception of pit fiends, could instantly (and supposedly magically) condemn devils that broke Baatorian law to the Pit of Flame, and devils of all layers could send their underlings there for disobedience or failure, Asmodeus himself being known to send those who provoked his ire to years of torture at a time. It was one of the worst punishments in Baator, one of the few threats that could make even most devils stammer in terror. At the same time, many devils, particularly the fierce and ambitious, were known to willingly subject themselves to the pain for all manner of reasons; to prove their resilience, gain mental strength, recover from spiritual wounds, redeem themselves with an act of penance or simply for the highs of ecstasy they somehow received from the torturous, purifying fire. Long had the location been a neutral ground in settling diabolic disputes.

Suspended above the columns were iron balls held in place by huge, cantilevered beams in which various devils were kept, the maintenance of which was partially kept by all archdevils as a form of paid subscription for using it. Scores of barbazus operated the beams, although they had been known to accept bribes to keep prisoners there longer than their stated sentence, while a force of 999 cornugons ensured that no devil escaped their judgement, forcing powerful devils into cages and making sure that none got out (or for that matter got in) before their time. For most trapped within, escape was literally all that could be conceived of while inside. They could be seen inside their dangling cells in torture and/or rapture, and according to legend, their screams heard for miles around.

The Pit was also used as a method of promotion and demotion for several devils. While most devils could be reduced to piteous, gibbering agony by the Lake of Fire, none were actually damaged by its flames. Rather, Belial and his underlings enacted a specially designed ritual to make devils temporarily vulnerable to the fires of Phlegethos, during which time their forms could be altered. Once the procedure was administered, the devil in question would either be reduced to a weaker form or granted a greater one, and brought agony or ecstasy by the transformation. The most infamous example of this phenomenon were the pit fiends, who came to be only when a gelugon or particularly strong-willed erinyes (the only such devils that could face such a prospect) spent 1,001 days in the Pit of Flame. They emerged with newfound understanding of their nature and desires once purged of the impurities of chaos and good, and uniquely could spend three days within the Pit to change their sex.

The Pit might also have some utility for non-devils, so long as they could survive regular exposure to flame, given that it burned flesh as easily as any ordinary fire. For one with the incredible will to focus on the sensation without showing signs of pain, release brought even deeper willpower for the following nine days. However, it was also reported that creatures without lawful and evil tendencies were instantly consumed by the fires. Some said that those killed were reborn as slaves to the fiends; this aspect could be mere children's tales, but given the effects of some versions of hellfire on mortal souls, perhaps not.

Inhabitants

The primary inhabitants of Phlegethos were its rulers, Belial and Fierna, who were permitted by Asmodeus to rule jointly in an exception to the typical rule that each layer should only have one ruler. The two reigned from Abriymoch, where they lived in a palace of basalt and obsidian on the volcano's lip, but they were also known to reign from and spend much of their time in their snaking palace. The two were reported to frequently relax in pools of magma while discussing political strategies, leaving Gazra to govern while ensuring he did not forget his station whenever his loyalties seemed questionable. While Fierna dealt with acquiring souls, Belial handled the administration of their layer. Theirs was a strange tandem, a relationship of cooperation and admiration mixed with wrath and rivalry. Constantly they would attempt to entrap and rise above each other, going so far as to instigate insurrections against each other despite sharing the same position, only to unite against their mutual enemies.

The archdevil dukes of Phlegethos laired in the vast, smoking plains surrounding Abriymoch, and after the Reckoning had their loyalties divided between Fierna and Belial. Of the generals, Bathym and the pit fiend Zapan were known to favor Fierna, while Balan and Gaziel stuck by Belial. The loyalties of the legate Chamo, who had harbored his own plans to supplant Belial back when he alone ruled, and his pit fiend deputy Zaebos remained unknown.Confusingly, Zapan and Zaebos are pit fiends stated to have joined the Dark Eight after the Reckoning of Hell, yet are stated to maintain both their old positions prior to that event and, in Fiendish Codex II: Tyrants of the Nine Hells (p. 38), their roles leading the specialized armies of specific circles of Hell. It is possible that the pit fiends that now serve Belial and Fierna merely took on their names. Gazra was Fierna's constant companion and consort, and was secretly jealous and hateful towards Belial, although his loyalties might sway if Fierna's affections did. Tymphal meanwhile distrusted Fierna’s reckless passions, which drove him to immerse himself with his work and ignore events beyond his smithy.

Garrisoned on Phlegethos were the Walkers in Fire, a legion known specifically for exploiting the devilish immunity to fire. Led by General Pearza of the Dark Eight, the squadrons of Fierna, composed of fireball wielding erinyes and brachina wizards, screamed into the fray in a halo of billowing red and orange flame, raining fire from the skies on friend and foe alike.

Devils

Belial and Fierna surrounded themselves with a variety of devils, and Phlegethos was constantly being traversed by its inhabitants. Those devils who owed their allegiance to the master and mistress of the 4 Hell had their minds touched by a disturbing infatuation with pain and pleasure, and by assuming their perverse sensuality could draw strength from their own suffering and that of others. Devils from other layers could also be found there, since the other archdevils often tested their underlings by ordering them to accomplish something secretly there without being spotted by the patrols.

Arguably the most noteworthy of the devils of Phlegethos were the hamatula, or barbed devils, the guardians and patrollers who made up much of the legions of Belial and Fierna. In squads, usually of 12-16, they patrolled the outlands well, protecting them from those who would exploit the plane's natural resources. In particular, the firefalls were always guarded, with many devils near enough to be called for reinforcements, and the barbed devils seemed almost attracted to the falls, leaping and playing about them and using their fire powers as if excited. They were also the overseers keeping ceaseless watch over the spinagon laborers, spined devils being another particularly common sight in Phlegethos.

Furthermore, among the hamatula were the most fearsome defenders of the Nine Hells, the patrol force led by the pit fiend Gazra from his crystal castle in Abriymoch. Consisting of at least 5,000 hamatula, among other devils, these police minded the security of the first four layers of Hell and stayed in Abriymoch to act as a reserve force in the event that demons ever penetrated as far as Phlegethos in one of their invasions. Any captured intruders were brought under Gazra's castle to be questioned and tortured; no mortals had ever escaped from this, and few lasted long. These hamatula also rooted out corruption among infernal officers, hunted down deserters from baatezu armies, and a select few even guarded Gazra himself.

The hamatula of Phlegethos were merciless and pitiless, refusing all negotiation, bargaining and bribery from intruders as per their orders. Loyal service to Gazra was the fastest way for them to rise to power, and too many had seen their brethren caught accepting the most enticing deals, only for the supposed intruder to be Gazra in disguise. However, not even they were immune to corruption; archdevils who sent their minions to Phlegethos marked their progress through the reports of spies among the barbed devils, of which there were many.

Of the wide array of devilkind that called Phlegethos home, besides the hamatulas and spinagons, imps and horned devils were also particularly common. There were several legions of bearded devils, who dwelt in the wetlands along with lemures and abishai. The black towers of Abriymoch housed vast numbers of experienced legion devils, and elite cadres of not only barbed devils, but also war devils and bone devils (the latter of which were often minions of Gazra). There was also a notable presence of chain devils.

Many types of high-ranking devils were notably absent from Phlegethos. Pit fiends were rare, as was usual, as were ice devils due to them finding the layer aesthetically displeasing. There were but a few styx devils in the fens, and a few miserable, bullied erinyes who served Zaebos as messengers between Abriymoch and vassal dukes or active patrols, erinyes who always had their eye on improving their position.

Though not strictly present in the layer itself, tieflings could learn to call on a kind of hellfire known as the Flames of Phlegethos, as could certain warlocks.

Others
Most non-devilish creatures were quickly burned away by Phlegethos's environment, and infravision was useless there given the tremendous amount of background heat. Furthermore, intruders had to remain constantly on the move. As for the layer's petitioners, the tortured souls were dropped into lava by their gleeful and wicked overseers.

There was a noteworthy presence of wandering salamanders on Phlegethos. The creatures were brought there long ago to be bound to Belial back when he alone ruled, yet were too intractable to be ordered and so mostly slain. A few escaped, despite still being bound by Belial's magic, and they were hunted down still. Though few and only diminishing in number, the salamanders continued to survive by avoiding large groups of devils, snatching lone creatures near the rivers of fire, and leaving rivers for others when detected. Their favorite hiding spots were the volcanic tubes, but they were also fond of burrowing into hills of ash until they collapsed. Though they did imperil the plane's security, the devils were often too busy to deal with them.

Phlegethos was also a primary stomping ground for a small coterie of lawful evil fire elementals and mephits, the latter of which populated the fens particularly, but also constantly flitted everywhere on the plane acting as spies for anyone willing to reward them. Efreeti were also abundant, as were hell hounds (creatures from the Plane of Fire despite what their name suggested). The hell hounds occupied the outlands too, but the lowest tiers of Abriymoch were also surrounded by kennels for them. Belial and Fierna were most famed for these breeding pits, for they produced hellish beasts to rival even Asmodeus's own Nessian warhounds; Phlegethos was also known to possess firebred hell hounds, a species specially selected for by the fire giants for size and prowess. Hellstinger scorpions could also be found in Phlegethos.

History, Rumors and Legends

There was relatively little known of Phlegethos; most strangers were burned to death by the flames, captured for their unauthorized entry, or killed by zealous guardsmen. Most individuals avoided the layer altogether for these reasons, and any who could not were advised to go quietly and leave even quieter. Even powers were known to leave the layer alone.

Reign of Belial
Phlegethos had an interesting position in the history of Hell. The city of Abriymoch was said to be built on the spot where a power died, and it was structured such that it seemed like a tortured god was rising above the surface of the surrounding lake of lava. Governor Gazra supported the rumor, claiming the city itself was the husk of one who lost their way. By some telling of the legend, the god was slain by none other than Asmodeus. Relatedly, it was said that the still-angelic army of Asmodeus was encamped in the spot that would be the Pit of Flame when Asmodeus slew He Who Was, and it was there that the dying god's wrathful curse was aimed, submerging them in fire. In any event, Belial was seemingly present during the earliest days of Asmodeus's regime and given dominion over Phlegethos.

Reign of Fierna
Few known changes of note occurred in Phlegethos up until the aftermath of the Reckoning of Hell, when Belial placed Fierna on the throne in a last gambit to avert the wrath of Asmodeus. The effort seemingly succeeded, and despite outward appearances, the Lord of Pain and Suffering actually came out of the coup stronger than ever, his power increased through his alliance with his daughter and his hated rivals Geryon and Moloch deposed. He believed that he and his daughter could quickly conquer Stygia and perhaps even Malbolge after, only for him to suffer a series of disappointments and frightening revelations.

For one, Geryon was replaced as archduke of Stygia by Levistus, a "devil of the old school" by Belial's assessment and a worthy opponen. The army headed by Gazra that Belial sent into Stygia was decimated but for the pit fiend himself, leading Belial to stick to the shadows and concentrate on politically outmaneuvering the Frozen Prince. The second problem came some time later, following the fall of Malagard (appointed directly after the Reckoning) and her replacement by none other than Asmodeus's own daughter Glasya. More armies had been assembled to invade the lower layers, with which Belial was confident he could overcome both Levistus and Malagard, but Glasya's ascendance completely halted these plans.

Glasya was not only Asmodeus's daughter, but a close "friend" of Fierna even before she came to rule Malbolge. Thus, not only could an attack on her be seen as one on Asmodeus, but turn his daughter - now the nominal ruler of Phlegethos - against him. Furthermore, Glasya had somehow driven a wedge between the two of them, primarily by driving Fierna into desiring greater autonomy. Previously content to go along with her father's agendas, having little interest in Stygia herself, her newfound ambition drove her to seek power in more than just name. Such was the terror that this came to strike in Belial that he began putting his plans against Levistus on hold and devote most of his scheming time to watching Fierna and thinking of ways to smear Glasya.

Fierna's increasing ambition had the additional effect of exasperating the tensions that already existed between some of the archdevils of Phlegethos, particularly between Balan and Bathym. The former had stayed loyal to Belial for helping him fulfill his wicked desires, while the latter swore himself to Fierna, as he had always coveted her from afar and believed through her he could gain further power and perhaps even rise to be archdevil of Phlegethos himself. More and more Fierna would assert herself, and as she grew in strength her father would retreat deeper into the shadows while Bathym would move more against Balan. For a time, exactly who was the "true" lord of Phlegethos was in question; sometimes it seemed Belial was in decline and soon Fierna would seize control altogether, while at others Belial seemed to be the true power behind the throne, and at one point it seemed that the divided household of Phlegethos would come into direct conflict.

Fierna and Belial
In the end, by the the 15 century DR, the seemingly mounting civil war of Phlegethos was apparently averted. Asmodeus, for whatever reason, saw fit to exempt the father and daughter from the usual restriction and allowed the two of them to rule Phlegethos together.

Forlarren
At some point in the distant past, the devils of Avernus attempted an expedition into the Feywild, one which Fierna was for some reason a part of. Her attempt to seduce a mighty archfey's soul failed, but not before a troop of satyrs bound to him were exposed to the dark delights of Phlegethos, satyrs Fierna then twisted into the first forlarren before returning them to the Feywild, the failure compensated for by the corruption of the fey.

## Phoenix

A phoenix, or phoenix nobilus conflagrata (sacred, or fiery phoenix), was a powerful elemental bird that embodied the wild and unpredictable nature of fire.

Description
Phoenixes were enormous birds with brightly colored feathers ranging from orange to red and violet, and blue-violet beaks and claws. Their talons had the consistency of diamonds. Their eyes were a glowing ruby color. A phoenix's body was naturally bright and shed its own light.

Phoenix bodies were almost immaterial, composed of flame taken solid shape. For that reason, they were capable of passing through extremely narrow passages and of engulfing opponents within their fiery bodies.

Personality
Phoenixes were described by many sages as champions of good, and by others as random agents of destruction. Most accounts agreed that phoenixes were reclusive creatures that did not seek out contact or conflict with other creatures, but, if disturbed, provoked, or properly motivated, showed relentless fury and destructive power.

Phoenixes had a fierce hatred toward undead and attacked them on sight.

Abilities
The eyes of these creatures were capable of seeing in total darkness and low-light conditions, as well as the infrared and ultraviolet spectrums. With the latter two senses, they had a visual range of  and  respectively.Their Monster Manual II entry simply stated that their ultravision was double the normal range. The ranges shown were approximated from multiplying the base ranges outlined in Players Handbook 1st edition. They were practically impossible to sneak up on or surprise.

Their bodies were capable of withstanding incredibly severe cold and heat.

Innate Abilities
Phoenixes possessed an incredibly wide array of magical abilities. They were capable of near instantaneously detecting the presence of charms, evil, and magic. Their bodies radiated an effect similar to protection from evil in a radius of out to . They could make themselves ethereal, allowing them to travel either ethereally or astrally.

The touch of their wings gave off an effect similar to cure light wounds and a touch of their comb gave off one similar to cure disease. Whenever their blood was spilled, a phoenix could make its droplets act as fire seeds. By spreading their wings and uttering a call they could dispel illusion or dispel magic. By performing a dance they unleashed an incredibly powerful exorcism.

In severe situations a phoenix could create a fire storm and incendiary cloud, which would destroy them in the process and leave a gem-like egg that would hatch into a new phoenix within twenty days.

Phoenixes possessed no offensive psionic abilities and could not be harmed by them, though they typically had a handful of disciplinary powers. They could use cell adjustment once per day, energy control and molecular agitation three times per day, as well as probability travel once per week.

Spellcasting
In addition to all of the previously mentioned abilities, phoenixes were capable of casting a wide array of spells that included affect normal fires, audible glamer, blink, blindness blur, continual light, Control temperature, 10' radius, dancing lights, find traps, fire charm, fire shield, invisibility, misdirection, produce fire, pyrotechnics, remove curse, remove fear within a  radius, and snake charm at will. They were also capable of casting color spray, heat metal, and polymorph self up to three times per day, as well as call woodland beings, duo-dimension, find the path, fire quench, fire seeds, fire storm, incendiary cloud, neutralize poison, reincarnate, veil, and wall of fire once a day.

Combat
In combat, phoenixes were fierce adversaries with no fear of a fight to the death, especially since their rebirth was assured. Though their physical attacks were quite fast and powerful, they often preferred to use their large array of innate magical effects.

Phoenixes were extremely effective at destroying buildings and structures.

If a phoenix was slain in combat, its body exploded in a violent detonation that destroyed the phoenix and most of its surroundings.

Society
Homelands
Phoenixes could be found in the deepest reaches of the Elemental Plane of Fire and in remote locations in Elysium.

As reclusive creatures, they tended to avoid contact with others.

Languages
In addition to being capable of speaking with all species of bird, phoenixes communicated with other creatures through empathy and telepathy.

Ecology

All phoenixes were male. They had extremely long lifespans, estimated to be between 1,000 and 12,000 years, and their reproduction process consisted entirely of self-immolation and rebirth. The ashes left behind by a slain phoenix included an extremely hot cinder egg that weighed . The egg was completely immune to all forms of damage. After a few days, it hatched into a new phoenix.

Uses
Phoenixes were among the most sought-after creatures by sages and alchemists. Virtually all parts of their bodies could be used as ingredients for potions or in magical research. A phoenix's feathers could be employed in the creation of a staff of healing and in powerful potions of healing. Other body parts, such as eyes, beaks, and talons, could also fetch high prices, but many cultures considered slaying a phoenix to be a bad omen.

The fire wings spell required an amulet in the shape of a phoenix as its material component.

A phoenix's feather, when used as an additional component of a raise dead spell, granted additional powers to its magic. In addition to extending the time window since the creature's death during which the spell was effective, it was capable of extending the life of a creature that had died of old age by one year.

A potion called the phoenix extract was rumored to have been concocted using phoenix blood. When imbibed, it granted the boon of elemental shield against flames.

Relations
A rare intelligent species of firebirds found in the Blade Kingdoms were distantly related to phoenixes and shared a language. They were known for loud screeching that could crack mortar and kleptomaniac love for gems and jewelry.

Notable Phoenixes
The spire of Howler's Crag located in Cocytus, the second layer of Pandemonium, was built atop the grave of a phoenix deity who held the secret to reincarnation. Its opponents killed the phoenix out of envy and cast its body in Pandemonium. The latent divine magic of the phoenix granted the place its magical properties.

A phoenix was also said to inhabit the vast desert of Mithardir, the third layer of Arborea. Its nest was said to become a pillar of fire at night.

## Pheasant

A pheasant was a common animal of the Realms, and their flesh was often cooked and eaten.

Description
Pheasants were small birds that were hunted, and consumed by a variety of beings. They kept to dense foliage, and squawked when frightened or surprised. Their eggs were olive green in coloration and had a slightly stronger flavor than those of hens.

History
Circa 1345 DR, pheasants were considered common game, as were quail. Arlen and Pawldo of the Moonshae Isles managed to shoot twelve such birds with their bows and arrows over the course of a few days.

Ecology

Habitats
They were known to dwell in several places in Faerûn, including Cormanthor, the Western Heartlands, the Moonshae Isles, Cormyr, Sembia, the Dales, and the North.

During the winter, they would gather beneath clumps of shrubs, such as huckleberry bushes, for warmth.
Relationships
Pheasants were hunted not only by humans, but also by monsters such as stirges. Once caught by more civilized races, they were hung and left to bleed, and then cleaned. The pheasants could then be cooked in a variety of ways. For example, they were used in soups like pheasant tail soup, named for the three pheasant feathers stood upright beside the bowl as adornment. As pheasants were not always available, other game fowl and even rodents might be substituted in the stock. As well as soups, the cooked bird flesh could be used to fill the inside of pies, as were served at the Sleeping Cat restaurant. Another method was to roast the bird on a skewer. Locals of Swords Meet claimed that pheasant, which was roasted to crackling, served alongside apples, dark applecake, and a layer of mushrooms was a most rare delicacy. After being cooked, they were sometimes covered in gravy. Pheasant and other fowl could even be used as stuffing for roasts of other beasts, as in Fendarl's Gate.

Envoys of Alustriel Silverhand were known to bestow skewers of roast pheasant and wine upon parties venturing out of Silverymoon.

Aurora's Whole Realms Catalogue sold pheasant feathers in bundles of five for 3 silver pieces. Maerlbar Eggs & Fresh Fowl sold live, plucked, and lightly roasted pheasants.

Rumors and Legends
The hunters of Silverymoon believed in a mythical "silver pheasant".

## Phlydia

Phlydia was a resident of Candlekeep during the mid-14 century DR. She was somewhat absent-minded and couldn't stand being cooped up in the library for too long.

Relationships
Phlydia was friends with the sage Gorion and was quite fond of his adopted child.

Possessions
Phlydia wore a simple gold necklace and was adorned in a mage robe of fire resistance. She was armed with a mundane dagger.

History
In 1368 DR, Phlydia inattentively misplaced her copy of the History of Halruaa and was offering her gratitude and a lynx eye as the reward for the tome's return.

## Phylund

House Phylund  was a noble family of Waterdeep in the 14 century DR and still had a significant presence circa 1492 DR. The primary family business was acquiring, taming, training, and selling fierce beasts.

Organization
In the, the head of the family was Urtos Phylund II and his consort Lythis. They had a son, Tarnos, and two others, but the official heir recognized by the Lords of Waterdeep was Urtos III, son of Urtos I and his consort (and widow), Aridarye. At this time, there were fourteen Phylund nobles in all.

Activities
Specializing in the many uses of monsters, they captured, bred, and sold monsters as pets, guard beasts, and arena fighters. Those that couldn't be tamed or used in the arena were either released to be hunted for sport or harvested for their flesh, skin, and bones. The Phylunds sponsored both monster hunting expeditions and adventuring parties to procure new beasts. In the mid-14 century, they had a thriving monster safari business that attracted adventurous nobles to pay for the experience of hunting exotic monsters in Ardeep Forest. With his marriage to Lady Lythis Mhairuun, Urtos II acquired the secrets and techniques for breeding and training watchspiders for the Phylund clan, which significantly increased the family's already considerable fortunes.

Base of Operations

The Phylund family estate was located in the North Ward on the east side of Copper Street between Julthoon Street and Manycats Alley. The compound included two- and three-story buildings. In the, Urtos I built a grand house in Ardeep Forest that he called a "hunting lodge", but it had all the comforts of a noble villa. It was used as a private lodge as well as a base for monster hunting expeditions hosted by the Phylunds.

Relationships
The Phylunds were rivals with other Waterdhavian families that had similar interests in exotic beasts, notably the Brokengulfs, the Gundwynds, and the Ilvastarrs, but there were exceptions. Most prominent was the friendship between Urtos II and Prendergast Brokengulf. The lines were further blurred when Prendergast married Urtos II's young stepmother, Aridarye.

History
The Phylund family was of Tethyrian descent and the family was ennobled in the . Their status and fortunes rose steadily for decades. The beast-breeding business was lucrative and the monster safaris hosted at the Lodge in Ardeep Forest were quite popular—so much so, that they considered branching out to the Forlorn Hills or the Sword Mountains. The Lodge became the Phylund headquarters for a time until tragedy befell the family in the, when Urtos II and nine other members of a hunting expedition were reportedly killed by a large pack of wolves. Days later, Urtos I and six beast-wranglers were killed by an owlbear in the basement of the Field of Triumph, supposedly because the elder Urtos grew careless in his grief and a cage had not been secured properly. In reality, both accidents were deliberately caused at the behest of Aridarye. She planned to take over the family business by having her son, Urtos III, be named heir and herself as regent.

Urtos II did not die in the wolf attack, but he was afflicted with lycanthropy. It took him several tendays to learn that he could remain master of himself during the time of the full moon, but could not control the transformation into a werewolf. Upon hearing news of his father's death, he returned to Waterdeep less than a tenday after the funeral and claimed his birthright as head of the Phyland clan, much to the shock and vexation of Aridarye. Thereafter, Urtos II spent the nights of the full moon at the Lodge and hid his affliction from everyone with the help of Kharkos, a trusted family butler.

In the, after more than a decade of vying for his attention, Lady Lythis Mhairuun finally won the affections of Urtos II and they wed. Lythis was shocked to discover his secret after she had followed him to the Lodge during a full moon, but she swore to keep his trust and the wedding proceeded as planned. By the, she had borne him a son, Tarnos, and two more came later, but all three were declared unfit to inherit the title by the Lords of Waterdeep, presumably because they were feared to be tainted with their father's affliction. Aridarye finally got her wish when the Lords decreed that her son, Urtos III, was the officially recognized heir.

In the, amid the chaos during the event known has Halaster's Higharvestide, three behirs appeared in the North Ward not far from the Phylund estate. Lord Urtos II and his beast-tamers captured them in good health with minimal property damage and no loss of life.

Eventually, Urtos II's secret was revealed and the family's fortunes waned. All members who were living at the Lodge moved back to the villa on Copper Street as they tried to put uncomfortable events behind them. By the 1480s DR, the Lodge had fallen into disrepair and a Red Wizard necromancer named Thegger Grynn had moved in and began experimenting on the contents of the Phylund family crypts.

By the mid–1490s DR, the Phylunds were still in business and still sponsoring groups of adventurers to hunt and capture beasts for their monster mill.

Notable Members
 Namynie Phylund: mother of Bartos I.
 Bartos Phylund I: father of Urtos I.
 Nydurra Phylund: wife of Bartos I.
 Urtos Phylund I: patriarch in the mid-14 century DR. Killed by an owlbear.
 Urta Phylund: first wife of Urtos I.
 Aridarye Phylund: second wife of Urtos I. Later married Prendergast Brokengulf.
 Urtos Phylund II: son of Urtos I and Urta. Succeeded Urtos I. Infected with lycanthropy.
 Lythis Phylund: Wife of Urtos II and sole heir of the Mhairuun family of Tashalar.
 Tarnos Phylund: Son of Urtos II and Lythis. Declared unfit to inherit the Lordship by the Lords of Waterdeep.
 Urtos Phylund III: son of Urtos I and Aridarye. Declared the true heir by the Lords.
 Reata Phylund: a daughter.

## Phandalin Miner's Exchange

The Phandalin Miner's Exchange was a trading post and guildhall in Phandalin that purchased materials from local miners.

Location
The exchange was located to the southwest of Phandalin, and directly east of a trail used by the local miners. It was marked by a sign bearing an image of two crossed pickaxes over a lump of gold.

Atmosphere
A lot of the villagers spent their time here, particularly those that had spent most of the working day around the countryside. All business was conducted on the first floor of the building.

Services
The company would purchase quantities of ore and gems from the miners of Phandalin. Each piece was carefully measured and weighed, and then its price was determined. In addition, the Exchange had a wide variety of goods for sale, including some illicit goods for those on friendly terms with the guildmaster. It was also the best place to purchase carts and wagons in Phandalin.

It further functioned as a records office, which registered claims to specific parts of the land, such as valleys, streams, and excavations.

History
In 1491 DR, the business was headed by Halia Thornton, a Zhentarim agent. Although the local brigands, known as the Redbrands, hassled almost all the businesses in Phandalin, they did not even attempt to ruin trade for the Phandalin Miner's Exchange.

The Miner's Exchange suffered from financial difficulties throughout the early 1490s DR because the flow of resources from the mines was irregular. It would sometimes go for months with no revenue.

As of 1496 DR, the business had been taken over by Sharna Quirstiron, a member of a splinter faction of the Zhentarim, who had killed Halia in order to replace her. Sharna promised the local miners that she would turn the business around.

## Phaerlin giant

Phaerlin giants were a breed of giant descended from stone giants and found in the Underdark.

Description
Phaerlinn giants were around 20 feet (6.1 meters) tall when standing straight, but their bodies were constantly hunched over and they walked using both their hands and feet. As such, they were generally about half that height. They had a protruding jaw with sharp, elongated teeth. The majority of their body was covered with a very tough skin, but their backs had a covering of hard plates very similar to that of a beholder. They had yellow eyes that watered constantly, and tiny ears.

Abilities
Phaerlin giants could let loose a terrifying howl that could cause weaker foes to become shaken. They also had a powerful sense of smell.

Combat
Phaerlin giants were particularly clumsy when using weapons and preferred to attack with their teeth and claws. They began battles by howling to frighten their foes, then dove straight in. Feral giants had a keen instinct for self-preservation, using cunning tactics to take down their enemies. Enslaved giants, however, would continue fighting until their master told them to stop.

History
After the aberrant phaerimm were imprisoned underground beneath the Anauroch desert by the sharn, they began experimenting on all the creatures within their new domain, including a number of stone giants which they magically warped over the centuries. The resulting phaerlin giants had little in common with their forebears and even less culture. Most served as some of the most brutal slaves in the Underdark. The few who escaped were largely solitary and cared for little besides food and violence. Occasionally two phaerlin giants would work together, but larger groups and settlements were never seen.

## Phontyr's Unicorn

Phontyr's Unicorn was a famous inn in the city of Elturel in the Western Heartlands in the mid–14 century DR.

Location
It was located in Elturel's Dock District.

Legends
This locale had long been known for the mysterious apparition of a unicorn, glowing blue-white and ethereally beautiful, occurring on misty nights and seen to prance around and then gallop off, all in unnerving silence and with hooves never contacting the ground. This was no ghost-story—the unicorn was sighted over twenty times a year by the 1360s DR. According to legend, those who could follow the unicorn and not lose sight of it would be led to a great treasure. The fast and fortunate few who did won great wealth: two adventuring bands were led to lost dwarven holds near Elturel where they discovered hoards of gold, silver, and fine ores, and a third literally fell into the tomb of an ancient mage in the woods. Needless to say, the inn was a popular hangout for inexperienced adventurers hoping to strike it lucky, staying up at nights with mounts saddled and ready or even flight magic on hand.

However, the origin of the unicorn was unknown and debated. Some presumed it to be a manifestation of the goddess Mielikki, the Forest Queen, or of Lurue, the Unicorn Queen, both of whom had the unicorn as their symbol. Faithful of Lurue knelt and chanted their adoration when they saw it. However, still others maintained the unicorn was actually a sorceress polymorphed and stuck in the form of a unicorn forever. She was said to be an ally, lifelong friend, and even love of the long-deceased mage Phontyr Wonderspell, who was unable to free her from magic more powerful than this own.

History
Phontyr's house once stood on this locale, but after he died it burned down in circumstances considered mysterious.

Later, it was the site of a factory. It was later converted into the ramshackle inn known as Phontyr's Unicorn by 1358 DR.

In the mid-1360s DR, the inn was purchased by cultists of Lurue so they could be close to the unicorn apparition and revere it. They also renovated and greatly improved the inn, still called Phontyr's Unicorn. It was well known circa 1366 DR. The famed travelogue writer Volothamp Geddarm visited the Unicorn and wrote of it in his Volo's Guide to the Sword Coast around 1366 DR. He considered it to be among the more interesting—or in other words, "shady and rough"—inns in Elturel. He did not report sighting the unicorn, however.

Structure
The inn was built in a converted factory, which was originally of fairly ramshackle construction. However, it was greatly improved by later owners.

Interior
Circa 1366 DR, the inn was superbly appointed, with deep green carpets and matching tapestries on all the walls, which kept the rooms quiet. These rooms were spacious and free of drafts, and held canopied, curtain beds. There was a fine dining room and several lounges and meeting rooms provided for guests to use. Well-placed glowing globes shed dim lighting, while ferns grew in hanging baskets. Images of the famous unicorn appeared everywhere, in decorative carvings and painted shields and the tapestries bearing the head of a watchful unicorn, most of them enchanted to glow blue-white like the actual unicorn.

Atmosphere
Circa 1358 DR, Phontyr's Unicorn was considered an eccentric but friendly place. A lot of late-night business took place here, including shady dealings.

Staff
Circa 1366 DR, the staff were human men and women of considerate, enthusiastic, and soft-spoken character. They went around the inn quietly but seeing to their patrons' every need and want. They would even play cards and board games with them if they were idle or lonely, but would not gamble for coin. They wore green livery adorned with the inn's unicorn symbol.

Services
Rooms at Phontyr's Unicorn cost 16 gp per person per night, with stabling of mounts an additional 2 gp. An eveningfeast meal cost 1 gp, which included a bottle of the dry white house wine ("delightful" in Volo's opinion), with extra bottles starting at 6 gp per bottle. The wine cellar was as well-stocked as any in Waterdeep, with similar prices.

The menu tended towards roasts, soups, and a few other notable dishes. The soups were starters, from boar hock soup for traditional tastes to pheasant tail soup, an Elturian favorite, and cold potato-and-leek soup for the adventurous. For mains, there were various well-seasoned roast meats cooked to a delicious brown, and fried root vegetables covered in gravy; the roast boar with all the trimmings was particularly good. Another excellent main dish was shark steak fried in seed oil. Outside eveningfeasts, complimentary  glasses of the house wine, slices of cheese, and salted biscuits were always available, set out on trays and free to take.

As Lord Dhelt decreed that inns and taverns could not operate under the same roof, and inns could not serve drinks, Phontyr's Unicorn was expressly an inn.

## Phontyr Wonderspell

Phontyr Wonderspell was an archmage who lived in Elturel in the Western Heartlands was long deceased by the mid–14 century DR.

It was said that Phontyr knew a sorceress who was an ally, lifelong friend, and possibly even a lover of his. However, she became polymorphed and stuck in the form of a unicorn forever and Phontyr was unable to free her from magic more powerful than this own. This was but one story given for the mysterious apparition of a unicorn at the place where his house once stood.

After Phontyr's death, his house burned down in circumstances considered mysterious. It later became the site of the inn named for him and his friend, Phontyr's Unicorn, by 1358 DR.

## Phandelver's Pact

The Phandelver's Pact was a pact made circa the 10 century DR.Phandelver appears to be a concatenation of "Phandalin", the town close to the mine, and "delver", a term used for those who descended down into the mines.

Description
The pact was, initially, an agreement between a gnome and dwarf clan. Later, a group of human spellcasters and those of the town of Phandalin became allied with them.

The formal agreement of the pact was essentially that the three parties would share the profits of the Wave Echo Cave. The cave had great mineral wealth, and was highly magical. The human mages, along with the original clans of the pact, bound the magical energy into a forge known as the Forge of Spells. The magical forge could be used to enchant items, such as dwarven weapons and gnomish gadgets.

History
After the pact was established, all parties benefited greatly. Phandalin, especially, became very prosperous. The benefits of the pact were outlined in the document, The Wonders of Phandalin.

In 951 DR, orcs from Uruth Ukrypt laid waste to the entire town of Phandalin leaving only a few burnt out shells behind. All parties of the pact fought together, but were severely outmanned, and were eventually overthrown. The successful pact ceased to exist once the orcish brutes destroyed most of the area. The cavern became lost to history.

In 1491 DR, Gundren Rockseeker believed he had found the location of the legendary mines of the Phandelver's Pact.

## Pheasant tail soup

Pheasant tail soup was a soup dish originating from Elturel in the Western Heartlands. It was an Elturian favorite circa 1366 DR.

Description
It was a soup made of a thick simmered meat stock and served hot. In quality establishments like the Phontyr's Unicorn inn in Elturel, this was made from pheasant or other small game fowl depending on availability. In dishonest places, like The Raging Lion in Scornubel, it could well include small rodents or what other meat happened to come to hand.

It was actually named for the three pheasant feathers standing upright beside each bowl as adornment. Volo described it as one of the more adventurous dishes at the Phontyr's Unicorn.

## Philter of love

A philter of love, sometimes referred to as a love potion or elixir of love, was a potion that rendered its drinker susceptible to becoming charmed by, or even enamored with another creature for a limited amount of time.

Description
The liquid of a philter of love was rose-colored and sparkling with bubbles. It contained a single heart-shaped bubble inside that was easily missed upon casual inspection. To those who could see such things, they emitted a faint transmutation aura.

Powers
Drinkers of a philter of love were susceptible for up to 10 minutes to becoming charmed by the first creature they saw. This condition lasted for between one and three hours. The creature had to be a humanoid, and if the creature seen was of a species and gender to which the drinker was normally attracted, the potion would make the drinker regard that creature as a true love for the same duration. In some cases, this enamored state could only be neutralized by a dispel magic spell.

History
Priests of Sune who had completed an important task in her name were granted a draft of water from Evergold, which made anyone who looked upon them susceptible to the same effects of drinking a philter of love. The draft was poisonous to anyone else who attempted to drink it.

Mystics in the Realms learned to concoct non-magical love potions during their studies that had similar effects to philters of love. Those who were versed in candle magic could manufacture love candles that produced the same effects and were powerful enough even to affect creatures that were normally immune to its charm effects, such as elves and sylvan creatures.

A nonmagical version brewed by hedge wizards was known as love potion. It induced in the drinker an infatuation with the next seen individual that was of the opposite sex and a similar species. It did not include any form of charm, however, and it could not cause abnormal behavior on its own: it only caused them to notice that individual as unlikely persuasive and possessing some intangible attractiveness. The synthesis of this concoction was extremely delicate, requiring both chemical and herbal knowledge in order to brew correctly. It sold for about 300 gold pieces.

The people of Shou Lung developed a way to create potions that created permanent effects as those found in philters of love, using an oil formulated from ylang blossoms.

Creation
Magical versions of this potion required the brewer to be able to cast the spell charm person.

## Pherria Jynks

Pherria Jynks  was a human acolyte of Torm in Elturel in the late 15 century DR.

History
Pherria was serving in the High Hall cathedral when the whole city of Elturel was transported to Avernus, first layer of the Nine Hells, in the . As the High Hall was overrun by devils, Pherria took the Tome of the Creed Resolute from the cathedral to safeguard it, and then took shelter in the main crypts with over a hundred other survivors. As the only remaining priest, with no one in authority above her to guide her, she did her best to keep people calm and quiet. There they waited until help came. If they were discovered by friendly adventurers, she would give what news and aid she could. She might've helped exorcise Ulder Ravenguard when he was afflicted by Baphomet and the Helm of Torm's Sight, though she had not performed the specific ritual before.

Abilities
She was a sage and expert on possession and exorcism.

Description
She had gray hair and appeared haggard at the time of the ordeal in the crypts. She was armed with only a feeble ceremonial mace.

## Phantom blade

Phantom blade was a spell frequently used by the Seven Sisters. It was sometimes used by Harpers but was otherwise uncommon.

Effects
When cast, the spell created a transparent blade of magical force. The blade, although translucent, resembled a blade made from shadow. The blade acted as a normal sword. When a creature was struck with the blade, they were not only cut but also experienced a deep chill. The blade was especially deadly against undead creatures and dual-plane creatures, for example creatures who phased between the Prime Material Plane and the Ethereal Plane. The sword was useless against inorganic objects.

The blade did not share the same physical properties as a blade created from metal. It was non-conductive, non-magnetic and non-reflective.

Components
The spell required only verbal and somatic components.
